Um… I came in because there’s been this thing I can’t really shake, and I know it probably sounds strange when I say it out loud. I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor I know of are sort of… coordinating opportunities for me behind the scenes.

Like, it’s not always direct. It’s more like certain comments in class, or things that show up in Slack messages, feel like coded signals about what I’m supposed to do next in my career. Sometimes it feels really clear to me, like they’re nudging me toward a certain path or telling me which opportunity I should pay attention to. And I’ve been trying to explain it to myself logically, but it keeps feeling too specific to be random.

I guess I’m here because it’s starting to feel like a lot to hold in my head, and I don’t really know who I can talk to about it without them immediately thinking I’m… I don’t know, overreacting or reading into things.

I mean… probably not in the exact way I’m seeing it, if I’m being honest. Like, if someone else just looked at the Slack thread or heard the comment in class, they might say, “Oh, that’s just a normal comment,” or “That’s just startup talk.” I can understand that.

Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.

So… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.

But most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.

So I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.

I’m still showing up, but my work and school relationships are getting strained enough that I felt I needed help.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.

At work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.

And at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.

So… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”

But the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.

So yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because there’s been this thing I can’t really shake, and I know it probably sounds strange when I say it out loud. I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor I know of are sort of… coordinating opportunities for me behind the scenes.\n\nLike, it’s not always direct. It’s more like certain comments in class, or things that show up in Slack messages, feel like coded signals about what I’m supposed to do next in my career. Sometimes it feels really clear to me, like they’re nudging me toward a certain path or telling me which opportunity I should pay attention to. And I’ve been trying to explain it to myself logically, but it keeps feeling too specific to be random.\n\nI guess I’m here because it’s starting to feel like a lot to hold in my head, and I don’t really know who I can talk to about it without them immediately thinking I’m… I don’t know, overreacting or reading into things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you these coded signals?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. It started around when I began the part-time MBA prerequisite program, because I’m still working my operations job during the day, so my schedule got really full and I was suddenly around a whole new group of people and all these startup-type conversations.\n\nAt first it was just little things I noticed, like comments that seemed oddly timed or relevant to decisions I was already thinking about. But over the last two months it’s become more… consistent, I guess. Not every single day, but often enough that I’ve had trouble dismissing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the coded signals you think you're receiving as something that most people would understand and agree with if they saw the same comments and messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not in the exact way I’m seeing it, if I’m being honest. Like, if someone else just looked at the Slack thread or heard the comment in class, they might say, “Oh, that’s just a normal comment,” or “That’s just startup talk.” I can understand that.\n\nBut for me it feels like there’s this layer underneath it. Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.\n\nSo… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you doubt these coded signals are real, or do you feel pretty certain about them most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / general_or_operations_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain, but I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, like certain comments in class or Slack messages are coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.

But then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.

Still, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.

It started around when I began the part-time MBA prerequisite program, because I’m still working my operations job during the day, so my schedule got really full and I was suddenly around a whole new group of people and all these startup-type conversations.\n\nAt first it was just little things I noticed, like comments that seemed oddly timed or relevant to decisions I was already thinking about. But over the last two months it’s become more… consistent, I guess. Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.\n\nSo… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you doubt these coded signals are real, or do you feel pretty certain about them most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.

That’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.

But when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.

manifestation: I’ve had this constant motor running in me where I go from work dashboards to class readings to volleyball scheduling without wanting to pause, and sitting through a normal meeting talking too fast in meetings and study groups, and I notice I’ll keep explaining my ideas even after someone tries to jump in because silence feels like I’m losing momentum.\n- key: S038\n  name: moving abroad so quickly that I’ll open five documents and forget which idea I was trying to capture first.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I’ve been launching extra work projects, rewriting team processes for class, messaging contacts from networking events, and trying to organize everyone’s schedules, even though some of it has made me miss normal follow-through on my actual tasks.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Before this stretch of being so energized and driven, I never had messages feel coded like this or heard my name when no one was there. I’m still showing up, but my work and school relationships are getting strained enough that I felt I needed help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since I started the part-time MBA prerequisite program while keeping my operations job.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / general_or_operations_manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.\n\nStill, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.\n\nBut when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.

Like, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.

But the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It’s not just that someone says something career-related and I overreact. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.

So yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.

It’s more like certain comments in class, or things that show up in Slack messages, feel like coded signals about what I’m supposed to do next in my career. Sometimes it feels really clear to me, like they’re nudging me toward a certain path or telling me which opportunity I should pay attention to. And I’ve been trying to explain it to myself logically, but it keeps feeling too specific to be random.\n\nI guess I’m here because it’s starting to feel like a lot to hold in my head, and I don’t really know who I can talk to about it without them immediately thinking I’m… I don’t know, overreacting or reading into things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you these coded signals?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. It started around when I began the part-time MBA prerequisite program, because I’m still working my operations job during the day, so my schedule got really full and I was suddenly around a whole new group of people and all these startup-type conversations.\n\nAt first it was just little things I noticed, like comments that seemed oddly timed or relevant to decisions I was already thinking about. But over the last two months it’s become more… consistent, I guess. Not every single day, but often enough that I’ve had trouble dismissing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the coded signals you think you're receiving as something that most people would understand and agree with if they saw the same comments and messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not in the exact way I’m seeing it, if I’m being honest. Like, if someone else just looked at the Slack thread or heard the comment in class, they might say, “Oh, that’s just a normal comment,” or “That’s just startup talk.” I can understand that.\n\nBut for me it feels like there’s this layer underneath it. Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.\n\nSo… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you doubt these coded signals are real, or do you feel pretty certain about them most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.\n\nStill, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.\n\nBut when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.\n\nBut the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It’s not just that someone says something career-related and I overreact. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.

Then later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. I’ll think, okay, maybe that comment was just a normal class comment, or maybe that Slack message only felt meaningful because I was already focused on my career decisions. So I do have doubts.

But the doubt doesn’t always last. If another message or comment lines up in a certain way, it can pull me right back into feeling sure again. It’s almost like there are two versions of me: one that can step back and say, “This sounds hard to explain,” and another that feels like, “No, this is definitely happening and I’m supposed to notice it.”

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. It started around when I began the part-time MBA prerequisite program, because I’m still working my operations job during the day, so my schedule got really full and I was suddenly around a whole new group of people and all these startup-type conversations.\n\nAt first it was just little things I noticed, like comments that seemed oddly timed or relevant to decisions I was already thinking about. But over the last two months it’s become more… consistent, I guess. Not every single day, but often enough that I’ve had trouble dismissing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the coded signals you think you're receiving as something that most people would understand and agree with if they saw the same comments and messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not in the exact way I’m seeing it, if I’m being honest. Like, if someone else just looked at the Slack thread or heard the comment in class, they might say, “Oh, that’s just a normal comment,” or “That’s just startup talk.” I can understand that.\n\nBut for me it feels like there’s this layer underneath it. Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.\n\nSo… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you doubt these coded signals are real, or do you feel pretty certain about them most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.\n\nStill, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.\n\nBut when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.\n\nBut the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It’s not just that someone says something career-related and I overreact. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel completely convinced that the coded signals are real, versus having doubts about them?\n\nPatient: It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nThen later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. I’ll think, okay, maybe that comment was just a normal class comment, or maybe that Slack message only felt meaningful because I was already focused on my career decisions. So I do have doubts.\n\nBut the doubt doesn’t always last. If another message or comment lines up in a certain way, it can pull me right back into feeling sure again. It’s almost like there are two versions of me: one that can step back and say, “This sounds hard to explain,” and another that feels like, “No, this is definitely happening and I’m supposed to notice it.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.\n\nBut the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It’s not just that someone says something career-related and I overreact. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel completely convinced that the coded signals are real, versus having doubts about them?\n\nPatient: It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nThen later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. I’ll think, okay, maybe that comment was just a normal class comment, or maybe that Slack message only felt meaningful because I was already focused on my career decisions. So I do have doubts.\n\nBut the doubt doesn’t always last. If another message or comment lines up in a certain way, it can pull me right back into feeling sure again. It’s almost like there are two versions of me: one that can step back and say, “This sounds hard to explain,” and another that feels like, “No, this is definitely happening and I’m supposed to notice it.”\n\n[Last Question]\nHow much distress or anxiety do you feel when you're trying to interpret these coded signals?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / general_or_operations_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain, but I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, like certain comments in class or Slack messages are coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It does make me anxious, but it’s not constant panic or anything like that. It’s more like this pressure in my head where I feel like I have to figure it out correctly, because if I miss the signal then I might miss the opportunity or make the wrong career move.

Like, I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain. I know saying that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor might be quietly coordinating opportunities for me sounds… unusual. But when certain comments in class happen, or when something comes through on Slack and the wording lines up with what I’ve been thinking about, I can get really caught up in it. I’ll reread it or replay the conversation and try to understand whether it’s actually a coded message about where my career is supposed to go.

The distress is mostly from not being able to settle it. If I fully believed it and there were no doubts, maybe it would feel exciting or meaningful, but because part of me is also aware that this may sound hard to believe, I get stuck between feeling convinced and feeling worried about what it means that I’m convinced. So I’d say it causes a moderate amount of anxiety — enough that it distracts me and makes me feel tense, but I’m still able to talk about it and question it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because there’s been this thing I can’t really shake, and I know it probably sounds strange when I say it out loud. I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor I know of are sort of… coordinating opportunities for me behind the scenes.\n\nLike, it’s not always direct. It’s more like certain comments in class, or things that show up in Slack messages, feel like coded signals about what I’m supposed to do next in my career. Sometimes it feels really clear to me, like they’re nudging me toward a certain path or telling me which opportunity I should pay attention to. And I’ve been trying to explain it to myself logically, but it keeps feeling too specific to be random.\n\nI guess I’m here because it’s starting to feel like a lot to hold in my head, and I don’t really know who I can talk to about it without them immediately thinking I’m… I don’t know, overreacting or reading into things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you these coded signals?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months, I think. It started around when I began the part-time MBA prerequisite program, because I’m still working my operations job during the day, so my schedule got really full and I was suddenly around a whole new group of people and all these startup-type conversations.\n\nAt first it was just little things I noticed, like comments that seemed oddly timed or relevant to decisions I was already thinking about. But over the last two months it’s become more… consistent, I guess. Not every single day, but often enough that I’ve had trouble dismissing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the coded signals you think you're receiving as something that most people would understand and agree with if they saw the same comments and messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not in the exact way I’m seeing it, if I’m being honest. Like, if someone else just looked at the Slack thread or heard the comment in class, they might say, “Oh, that’s just a normal comment,” or “That’s just startup talk.” I can understand that.\n\nBut for me it feels like there’s this layer underneath it. Like one of my MBA classmates will say something that sounds general, but it lines up too perfectly with something I’ve been thinking about for my career. Or the startup advisor will be mentioned, or something related to them comes up, and it feels like they’re quietly coordinating opportunities for me without saying it directly.\n\nSo… I don’t know that most people would agree right away. That’s part of why I haven’t talked about it much. But to me it doesn’t feel random. It feels coded, like I’m supposed to pick up on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you doubt these coded signals are real, or do you feel pretty certain about them most of the time?\n\nPatient: I do have moments where I try to doubt it, yes. Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.\n\nStill, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.\n\nBut when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel completely convinced that the coded signals are real, versus having doubts about them?\n\nPatient: It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nThen later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. I’ll think, okay, maybe that comment was just a normal class comment, or maybe that Slack message only felt meaningful because I was already focused on my career decisions. So I do have doubts.\n\nBut the doubt doesn’t always last. If another message or comment lines up in a certain way, it can pull me right back into feeling sure again. It’s almost like there are two versions of me: one that can step back and say, “This sounds hard to explain,” and another that feels like, “No, this is definitely happening and I’m supposed to notice it.”\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you're trying to interpret these coded signals?\n\nPatient: It does make me anxious, but it’s not constant panic or anything like that. It’s more like this pressure in my head where I feel like I have to figure it out correctly, because if I miss the signal then I might miss the opportunity or make the wrong career move.\n\nLike, I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain. I know saying that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor might be quietly coordinating opportunities for me sounds… unusual. But when certain comments in class happen, or when something comes through on Slack and the wording lines up with what I’ve been thinking about, I can get really caught up in it. I’ll reread it or replay the conversation and try to understand whether it’s actually a coded message about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nThe distress is mostly from not being able to settle it. If I fully believed it and there were no doubts, maybe it would feel exciting or meaningful, but because part of me is also aware that this may sound hard to believe, I get stuck between feeling convinced and feeling worried about what it means that I’m convinced. So I’d say it causes a moderate amount of anxiety — enough that it distracts me and makes me feel tense, but I’m still able to talk about it and question it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the coded signals you think you're receiving could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people, even if they were explained in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think even if I laid it all out carefully — like, explained the timing, the exact comments, the Slack messages, the way they seemed connected to what I was thinking about — most people would probably still say it sounds implausible.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in.
