But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So if someone investigated it, they might just say, “These are normal class discussions” or “That Slack message was just about the project,” and I would understand why they’d say that. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain.\n\nStill, if I’m being honest, I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and the startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re trying to guide where my career is supposed to go without putting it plainly. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. I mean, I know if I just say it plainly — that I think one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that comments in class or Slack messages feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go — I can hear how that would sound strange to someone else.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. Because I don’t want to be someone who just assumes everyone else should understand it. I know it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m connecting dots that maybe other people wouldn’t connect.\n\nBut when I’m in the middle of it, it feels very real and very specific. Like it doesn’t feel random to me. It feels like there’s a message underneath what people are saying, like they’re not stating it directly but they’re pointing me toward something. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.\n\nBut the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It’s not just that someone says something career-related and I overreact. It’s the timing, the wording, the way it seems to connect to things I’ve been thinking about or decisions I’ve been trying to make. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel completely convinced that the coded signals are real, versus having doubts about them?\n\nPatient: It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nThen later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. I’ll think, okay, maybe that comment was just a normal class comment, or maybe that Slack message only felt meaningful because I was already focused on my career decisions. So I do have doubts.\n\nBut the doubt doesn’t always last. If another message or comment lines up in a certain way, it can pull me right back into feeling sure again. It’s almost like there are two versions of me: one that can step back and say, “This sounds hard to explain,” and another that feels like, “No, this is definitely happening and I’m supposed to notice it.”\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you're trying to interpret these coded signals?\n\nPatient: It does make me anxious, but it’s not constant panic or anything like that. It’s more like this pressure in my head where I feel like I have to figure it out correctly, because if I miss the signal then I might miss the opportunity or make the wrong career move.\n\nLike, I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain. I know saying that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor might be quietly coordinating opportunities for me sounds… unusual. But when certain comments in class happen, or when something comes through on Slack and the wording lines up with what I’ve been thinking about, I can get really caught up in it. I’ll reread it or replay the conversation and try to understand whether it’s actually a coded message about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nThe distress is mostly from not being able to settle it. If I fully believed it and there were no doubts, maybe it would feel exciting or meaningful, but because part of me is also aware that this may sound hard to believe, I get stuck between feeling convinced and feeling worried about what it means that I’m convinced. So I’d say it causes a moderate amount of anxiety — enough that it distracts me and makes me feel tense, but I’m still able to talk about it and question it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the coded signals you think you're receiving could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people, even if they were explained in detail?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think even if I laid it all out carefully — like, explained the timing, the exact comments, the Slack messages, the way they seemed connected to what I was thinking about — most people would probably still say it sounds implausible.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I know this sounds hard to explain, and I know that saying I’ve been completely convinced that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me sounds… I don’t know, strange, or far-fetched. Especially the part where I think certain things said in class or written in Slack are coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nBut when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel random. It feels meaningful and specific, like I’m supposed to pick up on it. Then afterward I can step back and think, okay, if someone else told me this whole story, I might have trouble believing it too. So yes — I do think most people would probably consider it bizarre or implausible, even if I explained all the details.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe in these coded signals at all?\n\nPatient: There have been times where I’ve doubted it a lot, but I don’t know if I can honestly say there was a clean period where I didn’t believe it at all.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll step back and think, okay, this sounds hard to explain, and maybe I’m reading too much into a Slack message or a comment someone made in class. I can kind of talk myself into thinking maybe it’s just coincidence, or maybe because I’m so focused on where my career is supposed to go, I’m making connections that aren’t really there.\n\nBut even then, it’s not like the whole idea disappears completely. It’s more like it gets quieter in the background. Then something will happen — a classmate says something that feels too specific, or the startup advisor uses wording that lines up with something I was just thinking about — and I’m right back to feeling like they’re coordinating something, like there are coded signals I’m supposed to understand.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s been a time recently where I had zero belief in it. More like periods where I can question it, and then periods where I’m completely convinced again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific system or set of rules that you're trying to decipher in these coded signals you believe you're receiving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like a codebook where A equals this and B equals that. It’s not that organized. But I do feel like there’s a pattern I’m supposed to pick up on.\n\nIt’s more about timing and wording and who says it. Like if someone in class says something about “positioning yourself” or “waiting for the right opening,” and then later the startup advisor uses similar language on Slack, it feels connected. Not just normal business-school language, but like they’re pointing me toward a specific direction without saying it openly.\n\nSo I guess the “rules,” if there are rules, are more like… repeated phrases, comments that happen at the exact right moment, messages that line up with what I’ve been thinking about for my career. And I start trying to figure out whether it means I’m supposed to pursue one opportunity, or avoid another one, or prove that I’m understanding what they’re signaling.\n\nI know that sounds very hard to explain. I’m not saying I have the whole system mapped out. That’s actually what makes it stressful, because I feel completely convinced there’s coordination happening between my classmate and this startup advisor, but I’m still trying to decipher what the signals mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have no choice but to believe in these coded signals, no matter what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a good way to put it. When I’m really in it, it doesn’t feel like something I’m choosing to believe. It feels more like I’m seeing what’s there, and if other people don’t see it, then they just don’t have the full context.\n\nI can know, on some level, that it sounds hard to explain. That’s partly why I came in. I know saying that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me sounds strange, especially when I say the evidence is things like comments in class or Slack messages that feel coded. I understand how that would sound to someone else.\n\nBut at the same time, when the timing lines up or the wording feels too exact, it’s very hard for me to just dismiss it. It feels like, no, this is the signal, this is what I’m supposed to notice. And then if someone says, “That’s probably just coincidence,” I can hear them, but it doesn’t really undo the feeling. It’s like they’re asking me to ignore something that feels obvious to me in that moment.\n\nSo yes, sometimes it feels like I don’t really have a choice. I can question it afterward, or feel embarrassed trying to explain it, but when it’s happening, I feel completely convinced that there’s coordination going on and that those messages or comments are pointing me toward where my career is supposed to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interpreting other everyday events or conversations as having hidden meanings or messages?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do… although I think it’s mostly centered around things that feel connected to my career or school, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s not that every single random thing on the street feels like a message. But conversations that should probably be ordinary — a class discussion, something someone says about leadership or “being ready,” or a Slack message from the startup advisor — can start to feel like they’re carrying another meaning underneath. Especially if one of my MBA classmates says something that seems to line up with what the advisor later says, or if the wording is just a little too specific.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, honestly. I know it sounds hard to explain out loud. But I’ve been really convinced at times that one of my classmates and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me, almost like they’re using these comments in class or Slack messages as coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nSo yes, I do find myself looking for hidden meaning in those conversations. I’ll replay them afterward and think, “Was that just a normal comment, or was I supposed to notice that?” And once I start thinking that way, it’s really hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interpreting other everyday events or conversations, outside of those related to your career or school, as having hidden meanings or messages?\n\nPatient: Not as much outside of school or career, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m walking around thinking every conversation or random event has a hidden message for me.\n\nIt’s more that the things connected to my MBA program and work opportunities feel charged, I guess. Like that’s where I get stuck. I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain, but I’ve been completely convinced at times that one of my MBA classmates and a startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me. And then certain comments in class, or Slack messages, or phrases that repeat between them feel like coded signals about where my career is supposed to go.\n\nOutside of that, I wouldn’t say it happens often. If someone at a coffee shop says something random, I usually don’t think it’s meant for me.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. That’s the honest answer. Part of me wants to say yes, of course I could stop believing it if I had proof it wasn’t true, because I know how it sounds when I explain it out loud. I can hear myself saying that a classmate and a startup advisor are coordinating opportunities for me through comments and Slack messages, and I know someone else might think I’m connecting things that aren’t connected.

But when I’m in it, it feels very convincing. Like… completely convincing sometimes. It doesn’t feel like a random thought I can just dismiss. It feels like the wording, the timing, the way one person says something and then the other echoes it later — it feels like I’m supposed to notice it. Like they’re trying to guide me without being direct.

So I guess I hope I could stop believing it, or at least not feel so pulled into it. But right now it’s hard for me to imagine fully letting go of it, because it feels tied to where my career is supposed to go. It feels important, not just like an odd idea. I’m not saying I want to believe it forever, but I also don’t feel totally able to just decide it isn’t real.

Like, I’ll sit there and tell myself, “Okay, maybe that was just a normal class comment,” or “Maybe I’m connecting things because I’m stressed and thinking about my career all the time.” I can make that argument in my head.\n\nBut most of the time, honestly, it feels pretty certain. Not in a dramatic way, like they’re announcing it, but in this quiet pattern that keeps repeating. One of my MBA classmates says something that seems a little too relevant, or a Slack message has wording that feels pointed, or the startup advisor gets brought up in a way that seems like it’s meant for me to notice. And then it’s hard for me to go back to thinking it’s random.\n\nSo I guess I doubt it intellectually sometimes, but emotionally—or instinctively—it feels real. It feels like they’re coordinating something behind the scenes, like opportunities or direction for where my career is supposed to go, and I’m supposed to understand the signals without them saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of receiving coded signals interfered with your ability to function in your daily life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, more than I wanted to admit at first. I’m still showing up to work and to class, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything, but things are definitely getting strained.\n\nAt work, my manager has started documenting things — missed handoffs, follow-through that wasn’t reliable, stuff like that. And I hate saying that because I don’t usually think of myself as someone who drops the ball. But I’ll get caught up trying to figure out whether something someone said is connected to the signals, or whether I’m supposed to be reading between the lines, and then I miss the practical piece of what I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd at school, my professor questioned my judgment on a group assignment, which really bothered me. I think I made some decisions based on what I thought people were indirectly pointing me toward, and then afterward it didn’t land well with the group or the professor.\n\nSo… yes. I’m managing to keep going, but it’s affecting my work and school relationships enough that I felt like I needed help before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals as something that sounds strange or unlikely to you when you're not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, when I step back from it, I can hear how it sounds strange. Like if I were explaining it to someone else — “I think my MBA classmate and this startup advisor are quietly coordinating opportunities for me through class comments and Slack messages” — I know that sounds unlikely. I know most people would probably say, “That’s a reach,” or “Maybe you’re reading too much into it.”\n\nBut the hard part is that it doesn’t feel unlikely when I’m in it. It feels very… organized, I guess. Like certain things people say line up too specifically with what I’ve been thinking about for my career, and then I feel like I’m being pointed toward a direction without anyone saying it openly.\n\nSo yes, from a distance I can recognize it sounds hard to explain, maybe even odd. That’s honestly part of why I came in. But at the same time, I’ve been completely convinced a lot of the time that there is something being communicated to me indirectly — like coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the idea that your classmates and the startup advisor are sending you coded signals is something that could be proven or disproven if someone else investigated it?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that a lot, actually. Part of me wants to say yes, like if someone else looked at the Slack messages or heard the class comments in context, maybe they’d see the same pattern I’m seeing. Because to me it isn’t just one isolated thing — it’s the way certain comments line up with what I’ve been thinking about professionally, and then the startup advisor comes up, or someone phrases something in this oddly specific way, and it feels like there’s coordination happening quietly.\n\nBut then another part of me knows it would be hard to prove, because no one is saying it directly. It’s not like there’s an email that says, “We are sending you coded signals.” It’s more like… the timing, the wording, the repetition. So I don’t know if it could be proven in the usual way. I guess I keep hoping someone could look at it and either confirm it or help me really believe it’s not happening, because sitting in the middle of it is exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the coded signals you believe you're receiving could be considered unusual or hard for most people to understand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do think most people would find it unusual, or at least hard to follow. So yes, I can recognize that most people might not understand it… but I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're interpreting from your classmates and the startup advisor could be considered bizarre or implausible by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most people would probably consider it implausible, or at least really hard to believe if they weren’t inside my head when it’s happening.\n\nLike, when I say it out loud, I know how it sounds — that one of my MBA classmates and this startup advisor are somehow quietly coordinating opportunities for me, and that certain things said in class or in Slack messages are actually coded signals about where my career is supposed to go. I know most people would hear that and think, “That’s not likely,” or “Why would they do that indirectly?” And honestly, I don’t have a clean answer for that.\n\nBut the reason I keep getting stuck on it is because in the moment it feels so specific. It starts to feel like there’s a pattern, like they’re guiding me without saying it plainly.\n\nSo yes, I came in because I know this is hard to explain and probably sounds bizarre to other people. But I’ve also been completely convinced at times that it’s real, and that’s the part that scares me a little, because I can step back and question it, but then when I’m in it, it feels like I’m seeing something I’m supposed to notice.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel completely convinced that the coded signals are real, versus having doubts about them?\n\nPatient: It kind of goes back and forth, but when I’m in it, I can be completely convinced. Like not just “maybe there’s something there,” but really convinced that my classmate and the startup advisor are coordinating something quietly, and that the comments in class or the Slack messages are not accidental — that they’re coded signals about where I’m supposed to go professionally.\n\nThen later, especially when I’m trying to explain it to someone else, I can hear how hard it is to prove, and I start doubting myself. It’s more like this pressure in my head where I feel like I have to figure it out correctly, because if I miss the signal then I might miss the opportunity or make the wrong career move.\n\nLike, I came in because I know this sounds hard to explain. More like periods where I can question it, and then periods where I’m completely convinced again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific system or set of rules that you're trying to decipher in these coded signals you believe you're receiving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like a codebook where A equals this and B equals that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… no, probably not directly. Or at least I don’t think they would say, “Yes, we’re sending you coded signals,” because that would sort of defeat the whole way it feels like it’s happening.

That’s part of what makes it hard to talk about, because if I asked them straight out, they could just say, “No, you’re reading into it,” or “That wasn’t what I meant,” and I know that would sound like the obvious answer. But in my mind, it feels like the point is that it’s indirect. Like they’re coordinating opportunities for me quietly, not openly, through certain comments in class or Slack messages or these repeated phrases that seem to line up around where my career is supposed to go.

So if they denied it, I don’t know that I’d fully believe the denial. I might think they couldn’t admit it because it’s supposed to stay unofficial, or because they’re trying to guide me without making it obvious. I know that probably sounds circular, but that’s honestly how it feels. It doesn’t feel like something they would just confess to if I put them on the spot.
